Assuredly for no other reason is everything costly, but that there is a great multitude of idle folk — who, if they laboured, some in tilling the earth and some in fishing the water, there would be the greatest plenty in the world. And of the lack thereof it will be needful to render account in the dreadful day of judgement."

## Chapter 115


"Let man tell me: what has he brought into the world, for which he would live in idleness? Certain it is that he was born naked, incapable of anything; hence of all that he has found he is not the owner, but the dispenser — whereof he will have to render account in that dreadful day.

"The abominable lust that makes man like the brute beasts ought greatly to be feared; for the enemy is of one's own household, so that one cannot go into any place whither your enemy comes not. Ah, how many have perished through lust! Through lust came the deluge, insomuch that the world perished before the mercy of God — insomuch that only Noah was saved, with eighty-three human persons. Through lust God overwhelmed three wicked cities, whence escaped only Lot with his two daughters. Through lust the tribe of Benjamin was all but extinguished. And I tell you truly, that if I should narrate to you how many have perished through lust, the space of five days would not suffice."

James answered: "O master, what is meant by lust?"

Jesus answered: "Lust is an unbridled desire of love, which, not being ruled by reason, bursts the bounds of man's intellect and affection — so that the man, not knowing himself, loves that which he ought to hate. Believe me: when a man loves a thing — not because God has given him such a thing, but as its owner — he is a fornicator; for the soul, which ought to abide united with God its creator, he unites with the creature. Wherefore God laments by Isaiah the prophet, saying: 'You have committed fornication with many lovers; nevertheless return to me, and I will receive you.' As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — were it not for the internal lust of man's heart, he would not fall into the external; for if the root be removed the tree dieth speedily.

"Let man content himself therefore with the wife whom his creator has given him, and let him put every other woman into oblivion."

Andrew answered: "How shall a man forget the women, living in the city where there is so great a quantity of them?"

Jesus answered: "O Andrew, certain it is that he who lives in the city shall do ill; for the city is a sponge that draws in every iniquity."

## Chapter 116


"It behoves a man to live in the city even as the soldier lives when he has enemies around the fortress — defending himself against every assault, and always fearing treachery on the part of the citizens. Even so I say: let him repel every outward offer of sin, and fear the sense — because it desires supremely things impure. But how shall he defend himself if he bridle not the eye, which is the origin of every carnal sin? As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — he who has not bodily eyes is safe from receiving punishment save only to the third degree, while he that has eyes receiveth it to the seventh degree.

"In the time of Elijah the prophet it came to pass that Elijah, seeing a blind man weeping, who was of good life, asked him, saying: 'Why weepest you, O brother?' The blind man answered: 'I weep because I cannot see Elijah, the holy prophet of God.' Then Elijah rebuked him, saying: 'Cease from weeping, O man, for in weeping you sinnest.' The blind man answered: 'Now tell me: is it a sin to see a holy prophet of God, who raises the dead and makes the fire to come down from heaven?' Elijah answered: 'You speak not the truth, for Elijah is not able to do anything of all that you say, because he is a man as you are. For all men cannot make a fly to be born.' Said the blind man: 'You say this, O man, because Elijah must have rebuked you for some sin of yours, wherefore you hatest him.' Elijah answered: 'Would to God that you wert speaking the truth! For, O brother, if I should hate Elijah I should love God, and the more I should hate Elijah the more I should love God.' Hereupon was the blind man greatly angered, and said: 'As God lives, you are an impious fellow! Can God then be loved while hating the prophets of God? Begone forthwith, for I will not listen to you any longer!' Elijah answered: 'Brother, now may you see with your intellect how evil is bodily seeing! For you desire sight to see Elijah, and hatest Elijah with your soul.' The blind man answered: 'Now begone! for you are the devil, that wouldst make me sin against the holy one of God.' Then Elijah gave a sigh, and said with tears: 'You have spoken the truth, O brother, for my flesh, which you desire to see, separates you from God.' Said the blind man: 'I do not wish to see you; nay, if I had eyes, I would close them so as not to see you.' Then said Elijah: 'Know, brother, that I am Elijah!' The blind man answered: 'You speak not the truth.' Then said the disciples of Elijah: 'Brother, he truly is the prophet of God, Elijah.' 'Tell me,' said the blind man, 'if he be the prophet — of what stock am I, and how became I blind?'"

## Chapter 117


"Elijah answered: 'you are of the tribe of Levi; and because you, on entering the temple of God, didst look lustfully upon a woman — you being near the sanctuary — our God took away your sight.' Then the blind man weeping said: 'Pardon me, O holy prophet of God, for I have sinned in speaking with you; for if I had seen you I should not have sinned.' Elijah answered: 'May our God pardon you, O brother — for as for me, I know that you have told me the truth — seeing that the more I hate myself the more I love God; and if you sawest me you wouldst quiet your desire, which is not pleasing to God. For Elijah is not your creator, but God. Wherefore, as far as you are concerned, I am the devil,' said Elijah weeping, 'because I turn you aside from your creator. Weep then, O brother, because you have not that light which would make you see the true from the false; for if you hadst had that, you wouldst not have despised my doctrine. Wherefore I say to you, that many desire to see me, and come from far to see me, who despise my words. Wherefore it were better for them, for their salvation, not to have eyes; seeing that everyone that finds pleasure in the creature, be he who he may, and seeketh not to find pleasure in God, has made an idol in his heart, and has forsaken God.'"

Then said Jesus, sighing: "Have you understood all that Elijah has said?"

The disciples answered: "In sooth, we have understood, and we are beside ourselves, knowing that here on earth there are very few that are not idolaters."

## Chapter 118


Then Jesus said: "You speak the truth — for Israel now desired to establish firmly the idolatry that they have in their hearts, by holding me for God. Many of them have now despised my teaching, saying that I could make myself lord of all Judea by confessing to be God, and that I am mad to want to live in poverty among the desert places, and not abide continually among princes in delicate living. O unhappy man, who prizest the light that is common to flies and ants, and despise the light that is common only to angels and prophets and holy friends of God! If then the eye be not guarded, O Andrew, I tell you that it is impossible not to fall headlong into lust. Wherefore Jeremiah the prophet, weeping, said truly: 'Mine eye is a thief that robs my soul.' For this cause David our father with greatest longing prayed to God our Lord, that he would turn away his eyes, that he might not see vanities — for truly everything that has an end is vain. Tell me then: if one had two pence to buy bread, would he spend them to buy smoke? Surely not — for smoke hurts the eyes and gives no sustenance to the body. Even so then let man do: with the outward sight of his eyes and the inward sight of his mind let him seek to know God his creator and the good pleasure of his will, and let him not make the creature his end, which makes him lose the creator."

## Chapter 119


"For truly, every time that a man sees a thing and forgets God who has made it for man, he has sinned. For if a friend of yours give you a thing to keep in memory of him, and you sell it or forget it — you have offended your friend. Even so does man: seeing the creature and not having the creator in memory — who for love of man has created it — he sins against God his creator, by ingratitude. He therefore who shall look upon women and shall forget God, who for the good of man created woman — he will love her and desire her; and to such a degree will this lust of his break forth, that he will love everything like to the thing loved. Whence was born that sin of which it is a shame to have memory. If then man shall put a bridle upon his eyes, he shall be lord of the sense, which cannot desire that which is not set before it. And so shall the flesh be subject to the spirit — for as the ship cannot move without wind, so the flesh without the sense cannot sin.

"That it is then needful for the penitent to turn idle tales into prayer, reason itself shows, even if it were not a precept of God. For in every idle word man sins, and our God blots out sin by prayer. For prayer is the advocate of the soul; prayer is the medicine of the soul; prayer is the defence of the heart; prayer is the weapon of faith; prayer is the bridle of the sense; prayer is the salt of the flesh, which suffers it not to rot in sin. And I tell you that prayer is the hands of our life — whereby the man that prays shall defend himself in the day of judgment. For he shall heal his soul of sin here on earth, and shall keep his heart, that it be not touched by evil desires — to the vexing of Satan; for he shall keep his sense within the law of God, and his flesh shall walk in righteousness, receiving from God whatsoever he shall ask.

"As God lives, in whose presence we are: a man without prayer can no more be a man of good works than a dumb man can plead his cause to a blind one; than a fistula can be healed without ointment; than a man can defend himself without movement, or make attack without weapons; than one can sail without a rudder, or keep dead flesh without salt. For truly he who has no hand cannot receive. If man could turn dung into gold and clay into sugar, what would he do?"

Then Jesus being silent, the disciples answered: "No one would do anything else but make gold and sugar." Then said Jesus: "Why then does not man turn foolish chatter into prayer? Is time, perchance, given him by God that he may offend God? Surely not. For what prince would give a city to his subject, that he should make war upon him? As God lives: if man knew how the soul is transformed by vain talking, he would sooner bite off his tongue with his teeth than talk. O wretched world! For today men assemble not together to pray, but in the porches of the temple and in the very temple Satan has the sacrifice of vain talk — and, what is worse, of things which I cannot speak of without shame."

## Chapter 120


"The fruit of vain talking is this: that it weakens the understanding, so that it is not fit to receive the truth — even as a horse accustomed to carry an ounce of cotton cannot carry a hundred pounds of stone.

"But worse is the man that spends his time in jests. When he shall go to prayer, Satan will put into his memory those same jests — so that, when he ought to weep over his sins, to move God to mercy and receive pardon for his sins, laughing he provokes God to anger, who shall scourge him and reprove him. Woe therefore to them that jest and talk vainly! But if our God holds in abomination them that jest and talk in vain, in what account will he hold them that murmur and slander their neighbour? And in what plight are they that make sinning their trade, as business supremely needful? O unclean world — I cannot conceive how sorely you shall be punished by God! He then who would do penance, I tell you, must give his words at the price of gold."

His disciples answered: "Now who will buy a man's words at the price of gold? Surely no one! And how shall he do penance? It is certain that he will become miserly!"

Jesus answered: "Your heart is so heavy that I cannot lift it up — wherefore in every word I must tell you the meaning. But give thanks to God, who has given you grace to know the mysteries of God. I do not say that the penitent should sell his talk; I say that when he talks, let him reckon that he is throwing away gold. For, doing this — even as gold is spent upon necessary things — so shall he talk when it shall be needful to talk; and even as no one spends gold upon a thing that harms the body, so let him not talk of a thing that would do harm to his soul."

## Chapter 121


"When the governor has taken a prisoner whom he examines, and the clerk writes it down — tell me, how does such a man talk?"

The disciples answered: "He talks with fear and to the point, so as not to give suspicion of himself; and he is careful not to say a thing that may displease the governor, but seeketh to say somewhat whereby he may be set free."

Then answered Jesus: "This ought the penitent to do, then, that he lose not his soul. For God has given to every man two angels for recorders — the one writing the good and the other the evil that the man does. If then a man would receive mercy, let him measure his talk more strictly than gold is measured."

## Chapter 122


"As for avarice, let it be turned into almsgiving. Truly I say to you, that even as the plummet has for its end the centre, so the miser has hell for his end — for to the miser it is impossible to possess any good in paradise. Know you wherefore? For I will tell you. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: the miser, though he be silent with his tongue, says by his works, 'There is no other God but me.' For all that he has he is fain to spend at his own pleasure, not regarding his beginning and his end — that he is born naked, and dying leaves all.

"Now tell me: if Herod should give you a garden to keep, and you would order it as owners — sending no fruit to Herod, and when Herod should send for fruit you drove away his messengers — tell me, would you make yourselves kings over that garden? Surely yes. Now I tell you that even so the miserly man makes himself God over the riches that God has given him.

"Avarice is a thirst of the sense, which, having lost God through sin — because it lives on pleasure — and being unable to delight itself in God, who is hidden from it, goes about gathering temporal things, which it holds as its good; and it grows the stronger, the more it sees itself deprived of God.

"Wherefore the conversion of the sinner is from God, who gives the grace of repentance — as our father David said: 'This change comes from the right hand of God.'

"And I must tell you what man is, if you would know how penance ought to be done. And therefore today let us give thanks to God, who has given us grace to make known his will by my speaking."

Whereupon, lifting up his hands, he prayed, saying: "Lord God almighty and merciful, who in mercy have created us, giving us the rank of men, your servants, with the faith of your true messenger — we thank you for every benefit of yours, and we would worship you alone all the days of our life: weeping over our sins, making prayer and alms, fasting and studying your word, teaching them that are ignorant of your will, suffering from the world for love of you, and laying down our life to death to serve you. Save us, O Lord, from Satan, from the flesh, and from the world — even as you savedst your elect: for love of your own self, and for love of your messenger for whom you didst create us, and for love of all your saints and prophets."

The disciples answered ever: "So be it; so be it, O Lord; so be it, O our merciful God."

## Chapter 123


When day came — Friday morning, early — Jesus, after the prayer, called his disciples together and said to them: "Let us sit down. For even as today God created man of the clay of the earth, even so will I tell you what man is, if it please God."

When all were seated, Jesus said again: "Our God, to show his creatures his goodness and mercy, and his omnipotence, with his liberality and his justice, made a composition of four things, each contrary to the other, and united them in one final object — which is man. And these are the earth, the water, the air, and the fire, so that each might temper the extreme of the other. And of these four things he made a vessel, which is the body of man — of flesh, bones, blood, marrow, and skin, with nerves and veins, and with all his inward parts. In it God placed the soul and the sense, as two hands of this life: giving for lodging to the sense every part of the body, wherein it spread itself like oil; and to the soul he gave for lodging the heart, where, united with the sense, it rules the whole life.

"God having thus created man, he put into man a light which is called reason, which was to unite the flesh, the sense, and the soul in one end — to work for the service of God. Whereupon, this work being placed in paradise, and reason being seduced by the sense through the working of Satan, the flesh lost its rest, the sense lost the delight wherein it lives, and the soul lost its beauty.

"Man being left in such a plight, the sense — which finds no rest in labour but seeks delight — not being curbed by reason, follows the light which the eyes show it. Whence, the eyes not being able to see anything but vanity, it deceives itself, and so, choosing earthly things, it sins. Wherefore it is necessary that by the mercy of God the reason of man be enlightened anew, to know good from evil and the true delight; which, when he knows it, the sinner is converted to penitence. Therefore I tell you plainly that if God our Lord does not enlighten the heart of man, the reasonings of men are of no avail."

John answered: "Then to what end does the speech of men serve?"

Jesus answered: "Man as man avails nothing to convert man to penitence; but man as a means which God uses converts man. Wherefore, God working in a secret fashion in man for man's salvation, every man ought to be listened to, so that among all may be received him in whom God speaks to us."

## Chapter 124


James answered: "O Master, if by chance there should come a false prophet and a lying teacher, pretending to instruct us, what ought we to do?"

Jesus answered in parable: "A man goes with a net to fish, in which he catches many fishes — but those that are bad he throws away. A man went out to sow, but only the grain that falls on good ground bears fruit. Even so ought you to do: listen to everyone, and receive only the truth — for the truth alone bears fruit of eternal life."

Andrew answered: "Now how shall the truth be known?"

Jesus answered: "Everything that conforms to the book of Moses, that receive for true. For God is one, the truth is one; whence it follows that the doctrine is one, and the meaning of the doctrine is one — and therefore the faith is one. I tell you plainly that if the truth had not been erased from the book of Moses, God would not have given to David our father the second. And if the book of David had not been contaminated, God would not have committed the Gospel to me. For the Lord our God is unchangeable, and has spoken but one message to all men. Wherefore, when the messenger of God shall come, he will come to purify all that the ungodly have contaminated in my book."

Answered he who writes: "O Master, what shall a man do when the law shall be found contaminated and the false prophet shall speak?"

Jesus answered: "Great is your question, O Barnabas. Wherefore I tell you that in such a time few are saved, for men do not consider their end, which is God. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands — every doctrine that turns man aside from his end, which is God, is a most evil doctrine. Wherefore you shall consider three things in doctrine — that is, love towards God, pity towards your neighbour, and hatred towards yourself, who have offended God and offend him every day. Wherefore every doctrine contrary to these three heads, flee it — for it is most evil."

## Chapter 125


"I will return now to avarice. And I tell you that when the sense would gather a thing, or tenaciously keep it, let reason say: 'Such a thing will have an end.' It is certain that if it will have an end, it is madness to love it. Wherefore one must love and keep that which will have no end.

"Let avarice then be turned into alms, giving rightly what was wrongly gathered. And let him see to it that what the right hand gives, the left hand does not know. For the hypocrites, when they give alms, want to be seen and praised by the world. But in truth they are vain — for whom a man works, from him he receives his wages. If then a man would receive anything from God, he must serve God.

"And see to it that when you give alms, you consider that you are giving to God all that you give for love of God. So do not be slow to give, and give of the best of what you have, for love of God.

"Tell me: would you want to receive anything bad from God? No, certainly — O dust and ashes! Then how is there faith in you, if you would give anything bad for love of God?

"It would be better to give nothing than to give a bad thing. For in not giving you will have some excuse according to the world — but in giving a worthless thing and keeping the best for yourselves, what will be the excuse?

"And this is all I have to say to you concerning penitence."

Bartholomew answered: "How long ought penitence to last?"

Jesus answered: "As long as a man is in a state of sin he ought always to repent and do penance for it. So as human life always sins, it ought always to do penance — unless you would make more account of your shoes than of your soul, since every time your shoes burst, you mend them."

## Chapter 126


Jesus, having called his disciples together, sent them two by two through the region of Israel, saying: "Go and preach even as you have heard." And they, bowing down, he laid his hand upon their heads, saying: "In the name of God, give health to the sick, cast out the demons, and undeceive Israel concerning me, telling them what I said before the high priest."

So they all departed — except him who writes, with James and John — and went through all Judea, preaching penitence as Jesus had told them, healing every kind of sickness, so that in Israel the words of Jesus were confirmed: that God is one, and Jesus is prophet of God.

When they saw so great a multitude doing what Jesus did in healing the sick — but the sons of the devil found another way to persecute Jesus, and these were the priests and the scribes. So they began to say that Jesus aspired to the kingship over Israel, but feared the common people; so they plotted secretly against Jesus.

When they had gone through Judea, the disciples returned to Jesus, who received them as a father receives his sons, saying: "Tell me, how has the Lord our God worked? Truly I have seen Satan fall under your feet, and you trample him as the vinedresser tramples the grape."

They answered: "O master, we have healed countless sick folk, and have cast out many demons that tormented men."

Jesus said: "God pardon you, O brethren — for you have sinned in saying 'We have healed,' seeing that God has done it all."

Then they said: "We have spoken like fools. Teach us, then, how we ought to speak."

Jesus answered: "In every good work say 'God has wrought it,' and in every bad one say 'I have sinned.'"

"So we will do," said the disciples.

Then Jesus said: "Now what does Israel say, having seen God do by the hands of so many men what God has done by my hands?"

The disciples answered: "They say that God is alone, and that you are prophet of God."

Jesus answered with a joyful face: "Blessed be the holy name of God, who has not despised the desire of me his servant." And when he had said this, they went to take rest.

## Chapter 127


Jesus departed from the desert and entered into Jerusalem. So all the people ran to the temple to see him. Then, after the reading of the psalms, Jesus went up upon the pinnacle where the scribe used to go up, and, having made a sign of silence with his hand, said: "Blessed be the holy name of God, O brethren, who has created us of the clay of the earth, and not of flaming spirit. For when we sin, we find mercy with God — which Satan will never find, because through his pride he is incorrigible, always saying that he is noble because he is flaming spirit.

"Have you heard, brethren, what our father David says of our God — that he has remembered that we are dust, and that our spirit goes and does not return? That is why he has had mercy on us. Blessed are they that know these words, for they will not sin against their Lord forever — for after the sin they repent, and so their sin does not last. Woe to them that exalt themselves, for they shall be humbled down to the burning coals of hell.

"Tell me, brethren: what is the cause for exalting oneself? Is there any good thing here upon earth? No, certainly — for as Solomon, prophet of God, says: everything that is under the sun is vanity. But if the things of the world give us no cause to exalt ourselves in our heart, much less does our life give us cause — for it is burdened with many miseries, since all the creatures below man fight against us. O how many has the burning heat of summer slain! O how many has the frost and cold of winter slain! How many have been slain by lightning and hail! How many have been drowned in the sea by the fury of the winds! How many have died of pestilence, of famine — or because they were devoured by wild beasts, bitten by serpents, choked by food! O unhappy man, who exalts himself, with so many counterweights, besieged by all the creatures in every place!

"But what shall I say of the flesh and the sense, that desire only iniquity; of the world, that offers nothing but sin; of the reprobates, who, serving Satan, persecute whoever wants to live according to the law of God? Certain it is, brethren, that if man — as our father David says — with his eyes considered eternity, he would not sin.

"To exalt oneself in one's heart is nothing but to shut up the pity and mercy of God, so that he does not pardon him. For our father David says that our God has remembered that we are dust, and that our spirit goes and does not return. Whoever exalts himself, then, denies that he is dust; and so, not knowing his need, he does not ask for help — and that is how he angers God his helper. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: God would pardon Satan, if Satan knew his own misery and asked mercy of his Creator, who is blessed forever."

## Chapter 128


Accordingly, brethren, I, a man, dust and clay, that walk upon the earth, say to you: Do penance and know your sins. I say, brethren, that Satan by means of the Roman soldiery deceived you when you said that I was God. Wherefore, beware that you believe them not, seeing they are fallen under the curse of God, serving the false and lying gods; even as our father David invokes a curse upon them, saying: "The gods of the nations are silver and gold, the work of their hands; that have eyes and see not, have ears and hear not, have noses and smell not, have a mouth and eat not, have a tongue and speak not, have hands and touch not, have feet and walk not." Wherefore said David our father, praying our living God: "Like to them be they that make them and they that trust in them."

O pride unheard-of, this of man, who, being created by God out of earth, forgets his condition and would fain make God at his own pleasure! Wherein he silently mocks God, as though he should say: "There is no use in serving God." For so do their works show. To this did Satan desire to reduce you, O brethren, in making you believe me to be God; because, I not being able to create a fly, and being passible and mortal, I can give you nothing of use, seeing that I myself have need of everything. How, then, could I help you in all things, as it is proper to God to do?

Shall we, then, who have for our God the great God who has created the universe with his word, mock at the Gentiles and their gods?

There were two men who came up here into the temple to pray: the one was a Pharisee and the other a publican. The Pharisee drew nigh to the sanctuary, and praying with his face uplifted said: "I give you thanks, O Lord my God, because I am not as other men, sinners, who do every wickedness, and particularly as this publican; for I fast twice in the week and give tithes of all I possess."

The publican remained afar off, bowed down to the earth, and beating his breast he said with bent head: "Lord, I am not worthy to look upon the heaven nor upon your sanctuary, for I have sinned much; have mercy upon me!" Truly I say to you, the publican went down from the temple in better case than the Pharisee, for that our God justified him, forgiving him all his sin. But the Pharisee went down in worse case than the publican, because our God rejected him, having his works in abomination.

## Chapter 129


Shall the axe, perchance, boast itself at having cut down the forest where a man has made a garden? Nay, assuredly, for the man has made all, and the axe, with his hands.

And you, O man, shall you boast thyself of having done aught that is good, seeing our God created you of clay, and works in you all good that is wrought?

And wherefore despise you your neighbour? Know you not that if God had not preserved you from Satan you wouldst be worse than Satan?

Now know you not that one single sin changed the fairest angel into the most repulsive demon? And that the most perfect man that has come into the world, which was Adam, it changed into a wretched being, subjecting him to what we suffer, together with all his offspring? What decree, then, have you, in virtue whereof you may live at your own pleasure without any fear? Woe to you, O clay, for because you have exalted thyself above God who created you, you shall be abased beneath the feet of Satan who lays wait for you.

And having said this, Jesus prayed, lifting up his hands to the Lord, and the people said: "So be it! So be it!" When he had finished the prayer he descended from the pinnacle. Whereupon there were brought to him many sick folk whom he made whole, and he departed from the temple.

Thereupon Simon, a leper whom Jesus had cleansed, invited him to eat bread.

The priests and scribes, who hated Jesus, reported to the Roman soldiers that which Jesus had said against their gods. For indeed they were seeking how to kill him, but found it not, because they feared the people.

Jesus, having entered the house of Simon, sat down to the table. And while he was eating, behold a woman named Mary, a public sinner, entered into the house, and flung herself upon the ground behind Jesus' feet, and washed them with her tears, and anointed them with precious ointment, and wiped them with the hairs of her head.

Simon was scandalized, with all that sat at meat, and they said in their hearts: "If this man were a prophet he would know who and of what sort is this woman, and would not suffer her to touch him."

Then said Jesus: "Simon, I have a thing to say to you." Simon answered: "Speak, O Master, for I desire your word."

## Chapter 130


Jesus said: "There was a man who had two debtors. The one owed to his creditor fifty pence, the other five hundred. Whereupon, when neither of them had wherewithal to pay, the owner, moved with compassion, forgave the debt to each. Which of them would love his creditor most?"

Simon answered: "He to whom was forgiven the greater debt."

Said Jesus: "You have well said; I say to you, therefore, behold this woman and thyself; for you were both debtors to God, the one for leprosy of the body, the other for leprosy of the soul, which is sin.

"God our Lord, moved with compassion through my prayers, has willed to heal your body and her soul. You, therefore, love me little, because you have received little as a gift. And so, when I entered your house you didst not kiss me nor anoint my head. But this woman, lo! straightway on entering your house she placed herself at my feet, which she has washed with her tears and anointed with precious ointment. Wherefore truly I say to you, many sins are forgiven her, because she has loved much." And turning to the woman he said: "Go your way in peace, for the Lord our God has pardoned your sins; but see you sin no more. Your faith has saved you."

## Chapter 131


His disciples drew nigh to Jesus after the nightly prayer, and said: "O Master, how must we do to escape pride?"

Jesus answered: "Have you seen a poor man invited to a prince's house to eat bread?"

John answered: "I have eaten bread in Herod's house. For before I knew you I went to fish, and used to sell the fish to the family of Herod. Whereupon, one day when he was feasting, I having brought thither a fine fish, he made me stay and eat there."

Then said Jesus: "Now how didst you eat bread with infidels? God pardon you, O John! But tell me, how didst you bear thyself at the board? Didst you seek to have the most honourable place? Didst you ask for the most delicate food? Didst you speak when you were not questioned at the table? Didst you account thyself more worthy than the others to sit at table?"

John answered: "As God lives, I did not dare to lift up my eyes, seeing myself, a poor fisherman, ill-clad, sitting among the king's barons. Whereupon, when the king gave me a little piece of flesh, methought that the world had fallen upon my head, for the greatness of the favour that the king did to me. And truly I say that, if the king had been of our Law, I should have been fain to serve him all the days of my life."

Jesus cried out: "Hold your peace, John, for I fear lest God cast us into the abyss, even like Abiram, for our pride!"

The disciples trembled with fear at the words of Jesus; when he said again: "Let us fear God, that He cast us not into the abyss for our pride.

"O brethren, have you heard of John what is done in the house of a prince? Woe to the men that come into the world, for as they live in pride they shall die in contempt and shall go into confusion.

"For this world is a house where God feasts men, wherein have eaten all the holy ones and prophets of God. And truly I say to you, everything that a man receiveth, he receiveth it from God. Wherefore man ought to bear himself with deepest humility; knowing his own vileness and the greatness of God, with the great bounty wherewith he nourishes us. Therefore it is not lawful for man to say: 'Ah, why is this done and this said in the world?' but rather to account himself, as in truth he is, unworthy to stand in the world at God's board. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, there is nothing so small received here in the world from God, but that in return man ought to spend his life for love of God.

"As God lives, you sinnedst not, O John, in eating with Herod, for it was of God's disposition you didst so, in order that you mightest be our teacher and the teacher of every one that fears God. So do," said Jesus to his disciples, "that you may live in the world as John lived in the house of Herod when he ate bread with him, for so shall you be in truth free from all pride."

## Chapter 132


Jesus walking along the sea of Galilee was surrounded by a great multitude of folk, whereupon he went up into a little boat, which drew off from land by itself; and it anchored near land, near enough for the voice of Jesus to be heard. Whereupon they all drew nigh to the sea, and sitting down awaited his word. He then opened his mouth and said: "Behold, the sower went out to sow, whereupon as he sowed some of the seed fell upon the road, and this was trodden under foot of men and eaten up of birds; some fell upon the stones, whereupon when it sprang up, because it had no moisture, it was burnt up by the sun; some fell in the hedges, whereupon when it grew up the thorns choked the seed; and some fell on good ground, whereupon it bare fruit, even to thirtyfold, sixtyfold, and a hundredfold."

Again Jesus said: "Behold, the father of a family sowed good seed in his field: whereupon, as the servants of the good man slept, the enemy of the man, their master, came and sowed tares over the good seed. Whereupon, when the corn sprang up, there was seen sprung up among the corn a great quantity of tares. The servants came to their master and said: 'O sir, didst you not sow good seed in your field? Wherefore then is there sprung up therein a great quantity of tares?' The master answered: 'Good seed did I sow, but while men slept the enemy of man came and sowed tares over the corn.' Said the servants: 'Will you that we go and pull up the tares from among the corn?' The master answered: 'Do not so, for you would pull up the corn therewith; but wait till the time of harvest comes. For then shall you go and pull up the tares from among the corn and cast them into the fire to be burned, but the corn you shall put into my granary.'"

Again Jesus said: "There went forth many men to sell figs. But when they arrived at the market-place, behold, men sought not good figs but fair leaves. Therefore the men were not able to sell their figs. And seeing this, an evil citizen said: 'Surely I may become rich.' Whereupon he called together his two sons and said: 'Go you and gather a great quantity of leaves with bad figs.' And these they sold at the price of gold, for the men were mightily pleased with leaves. Whereupon the men, eating the figs, became sick with a grievous sickness."

Again Jesus said: "Behold, a citizen has a fountain, from which all the neighbouring citizens take water to wash off their uncleanness; but the citizen suffers his own clothes to putrefy."

Again Jesus said: "There went forth two men to sell apples. The one chose to sell the peel of the apple for its weight in gold, caring nought for the substance of the apples. The other desired to give the apples away, receiving only a little bread for his journey. But men bought the peel of the apples for its weight in gold, caring nought for him who was fain to give them, nay even despising him."

And thus on that day Jesus spake to the crowd in parables. Then having dismissed them, he went with his disciples to Nain, where he had raised to life the widow's son; who, with his mother, received him into his house and ministered to him.

## Chapter 133


His disciples drew near to Jesus, and asked him, saying: "O Master, tell us the meaning of the parables that you spoke to the people." Jesus answered: "The hour of prayer draws near; so when the evening prayer is ended I will tell you the meaning of the parables." When the prayer was ended, the disciples drew near to Jesus, and he said to them: "The man who sows upon the road, upon the stones, upon the thorns, and upon the good ground, is he who teaches the word of God, which falls upon a great number of men. It falls upon the road when it comes to the ears of sailors and merchants, who, by reason of the long journeys they make and the variety of nations with whom they deal, have the word of God taken from their memory by Satan. It falls upon the stones when it comes to the ears of men of the court, for by reason of the great care they have to serve the body of a prince the word of God does not sink into them; so that although they have some memory of it, as soon as they have any trouble the word of God goes out of their memory, for, seeing they serve not God, they cannot hope for help from God. It falls among the thorns when it comes to the ears of those who love their own life, so that, though the word of God grows in them, when carnal desires grow up they choke the good seed of the word of God, for carnal comforts make men forsake the word of God. That which falls upon good ground is when the word of God comes to the ears of him who fears God, so it brings forth fruit of eternal life. Truly I say to you, that in every condition, when man fears God, the word of God will bear fruit in him.

"Of that father of a family I tell you truly that he is God our Lord, father of all things, for he has created all things. But he is not a father by nature, for he is incapable of motion, without which nothing can be generated. Our God, then, whose this world is — his field where he sows is mankind, and the seed is the word of God. So when the teachers are negligent in preaching the word of God through being busy with the business of the world, Satan sows error in the heart of men, from which have come countless sects of wicked doctrine.

"The holy ones and prophets cry: 'O sir, did you not then give good doctrine to men? Why then are there so many errors?' God answers: 'I gave good doctrine to men, but while men have been given up to vanity Satan has sown errors to bring my law to nothing.' The holy ones say: 'O Lord, we will scatter these errors by destroying men.' God answers: 'Do not do this, for the faithful are so closely joined by kinship to the infidels that the faithful would be lost with the infidel; but wait until the judgment, for at that time the infidels shall be gathered by my angels and shall be cast out with Satan into hell. Then the faithful shall come into my kingdom. Certainly many infidel fathers shall beget faithful sons, for whose sake God waits for the world to repent.'"

## Chapter 134


They who bear good figs are the true teachers, who preach good doctrine; but the world, which takes pleasure in lies, seeks from the teachers leaves of fine words with flattery. Seeing this, Satan joins himself with the flesh and the senses, and brings a great supply of leaves — that is, a quantity of earthly things — in which he covers sin, which, when man receives it, makes him sick and disposes him to eternal death.

The citizen who has the water and gives his water to others to wash off their uncleanness, but lets his own garments rot, is the teacher who preaches penitence to others but himself always abides in sin. O wretched man, because not the angels but his own tongue writes upon the air the punishment that is fitting for him! If one had the tongue of an elephant, and the rest of his body were as small as an ant, would this not be a monstrous thing? Yes, surely. Now truly I say to you that he is more monstrous who preaches penitence to others, but himself repents not of his sins.

Those two men who sell apples are — the one, he who preaches for love of God, and so flatters no one but preaches in truth, seeking only a poor man's livelihood. As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands, such a man is not received by the world but rather despised. But he who sells the peel for its weight in gold, and gives the apple away, is he who preaches to please men; so, flattering the world, he loses his soul — whoever follows his flattery. Ah, how many have perished for this cause!

Then he who writes answered and said: "How should one listen to the word of God, and how should one know him who preaches for love of God?" Jesus answered: "He who preaches should be listened to as though God were speaking, when he preaches good doctrine, because God speaks through his mouth. But he who reproves not sins, having respect of persons, flattering particular men, should be avoided as a horrible serpent, for truly he poisons the human heart. Do you understand? Truly I say to you that even as a wounded man has no need of fine bandages to bind his wounds, but rather of a good ointment, so also the sinner has no need of fine words but rather of good reproofs, that he may cease to sin."

## Chapter 135


Then said Peter: "O Master, tell us how the lost shall be tormented, and how long they shall be in hell, in order that man may flee from sin." Jesus answered: "O Peter, great is your question; nevertheless, if it please God, I will answer you. Know you, therefore, that hell is one, yet has seven centres, one below another. Hence, even as sin is of seven kinds — for as seven gates of hell has Satan generated it — so are there seven punishments therein.

"For the proud, that is the loftiest in heart, shall be plunged into the lowest centre, passing through all the centres above it, and suffering in them all the pains that are therein. And as here he seeks to be higher than God, in wishing to do after his own manner, contrary to that which God commands, and not wishing to recognize anyone above him: even so there shall he be put under the feet of Satan and his devils, who shall trample him down as the grapes are trampled when wine is made, and he shall be ever derided and scorned of devils.

"The envious, who here chafes at the good of his neighbour and rejoices at his misfortune, shall go down to the sixth centre, and there shall be gnawed by the fangs of a great number of infernal serpents. And it shall seem to him that all things in hell rejoice at his torment, and mourn that he be not gone down to the seventh centre. For although the damned are incapable of any joy, yet the justice of God shall cause that it shall so seem to the wretched envious man, as when one seems in a dream to be spurned by someone and feels torment thereby — even so shall be the object set before the wretched envious man. For where there is no gladness at all it shall seem to him that every one rejoices at his misfortune, and mourns that he has no worse.

"The covetous shall go down to the fifth centre, where he shall suffer extreme poverty, as the rich feaster suffered. And the demons, for greater torment, shall offer him that which he desires, and when he shall have it in his hands other devils with violence shall snatch it from his hands, with these words: 'Remember that you would not give for love of God; so God wills not that you now receive.' Oh unhappy man! Now shall he find himself in that condition when he shall remember past abundance and behold the penury of the present; and that with the goods that then he may not have he could have acquired eternal delights!

"To the fourth centre shall go the lustful, where they that have transformed the way given them by God shall be as corn that is cooked, immersed in the burning dung of the devil. And there shall they be embraced by horrible infernal serpents. And they that shall have sinned with harlots — all those acts of impurity shall be transformed for them into union with the infernal furies, which are demons like women, whose hair is serpents, whose eyes are flaming sulphur, whose mouth is poisonous, whose tongue is gall, whose body is all girt with barbed hooks like those wherewith they catch the silly fish, whose claws are those of gryphons, whose nails are razors, the nature of whose generative organs is fire. Now with these shall all the lustful enjoy the infernal embers which shall be their bed.

"To the third centre shall go down the slothful who will not work now. Here are built cities and immense palaces, which as soon as they are finished must needs be pulled down straightway, because a single stone is not placed aright. And these enormous stones are laid upon the shoulders of the slothful, who has not his hands free to cool his body as he walks and to ease the burden, seeing that sloth has taken away the power of his arms, and his legs are fettered with infernal serpents. And, what is worse, behind him are the demons, who push him, and make him fall to earth many times beneath the weight; nor does any help him to lift it up: nay, it being too much to lift, a double amount is laid upon him.

"To the second centre shall go down the gluttonous. Now here there is dearth of food, to such a degree that there shall be nothing to eat but live scorpions and live serpents, which give such torment that it would be better never to have been born than to eat such food. There are offered to them indeed by the demons, in appearance, delicate meats; but for that they have their hands and feet bound with fetters of fire, they cannot lay hands on what seems to them food. But what is worse, those very scorpions which he eats, that they may devour his belly — not being able to come forth speedily, they rend the secret parts of the glutton. And when they are come forth foul and unclean, filthy as they are, they are eaten over again.

"The wrathful goes down to the first centre, where he is insulted by all the devils and by as many of the damned as go down lower than he. They spurn him and smite him, making him bow down upon the road where they pass, planting their feet upon his throat. Yet he is not able to defend himself, for that he has his hands and feet bound. And what is worse, he is not able to give vent to his wrath by insulting others, seeing that his tongue is fastened by a hook, like that which he uses who sells flesh.

"In this accursed place shall there be a general punishment, common to all the centres, like the mixture of various grains to make a loaf. For fire, ice, thunderstorms, lightning, sulphur, heat, cold, wind, frenzy, terror, shall all be united by the justice of God, and in such wise that the cold shall not temper the heat nor the fire the ice, but each shall give torment to the wretched sinner."

## Chapter 136


In this accursed spot shall abide the infidels for evermore: insomuch that if the world were filled with grains of millet, and a single bird once in a hundred years should take away a single grain to empty the world — if when it should be empty the infidels were to go into paradise, they would rest delighted. But there is not this hope, because their torment cannot have an end, seeing that they were not willing for the love of God to put an end to their sin. But the faithful shall have comfort, because their torment shall have an end.

The disciples were affrighted, hearing this, and said: "So then the faithful must go into hell!" Jesus answered: "Every one, be he who he may, must go into hell. It is true, however, that the holy ones and prophets of God shall go there to behold, not suffering any punishment; and the righteous, only suffering fear. And what shall I say! I tell you that thither shall come even the messenger of God, to behold the justice of God. Thereupon hell shall tremble at his presence. And because he has human flesh, all those that have human flesh and shall be under punishment, so long as the messenger of God shall abide to behold hell, so long shall they abide without punishment. But he shall abide there only so long as it takes to shut and open the eyes. And this shall God do in order that every creature may know that he has received benefit from the messenger of God.

"When he shall go there all the devils shall shriek, and seek to hide themselves beneath the burning embers, saying one to another: 'Fly, fly, for here comes Mohammed our enemy!' Hearing which, Satan shall smite himself upon the face with both his hands, and screaming shall say: 'you are more noble than I, in my despite, and this is unjustly done!'

"As for the faithful, who are in seventy-two grades, those of the two last grades, who shall have had the faith without good works — the one being grieved at good works, and the other delighting in evil — they shall abide in hell seventy thousand years. After those years shall the angel Gabriel come into hell, and shall hear them say: 'O Mohammed, where are your promises made to us, saying that those who have your faith shall not abide in hell for evermore?' Then the angel of God shall return to paradise, and having approached with reverence the messenger of God shall narrate to him what he has heard. Then shall his messenger speak to God and say: 'Lord, my God, remember the promise made to me your servant, concerning them that have received my faith, that they shall not abide for evermore in hell.' God shall answer: 'Ask what you will, O my friend, for I will give you all that you ask.'"

## Chapter 137


Then shall the messenger of God say: "O Lord, there are of the faithful who have been in hell seventy thousand years. Where, O Lord, is your mercy? I pray you, Lord, to free them from those bitter punishments." Then shall God command the four favourite angels of God to go to hell and take out every one who has the faith of his messenger and lead them into paradise; and this they shall do. And such shall be the gain of the faith of the messenger of God, that those who shall have believed in him, even though they have not done good works, seeing they died in this faith, shall go into paradise after the punishment of which I have spoken.

## Chapter 138


When morning came, early, all the men of the city with the women and children came to the house where Jesus was with his disciples, and begged him, saying: "Sir, have mercy on us, for this year the worms have eaten the grain, and we shall not receive bread this year in our land."

Jesus answered: "O, what fear is yours! Do you not know that the servant of God, Elijah, for three years — while the persecution of Ahab continued — saw no bread, nourishing himself only with herbs and wild fruits? David our father, prophet of God, for two years stood eating wild fruits and herbs, being persecuted by Saul, so much that only twice did he eat bread."

The men answered: "Sir, they were prophets of God, nourished with spiritual delight, and therefore endured well. But how will these little ones fare?" — and they showed him the multitude of their children. Then Jesus had compassion on their misery, and said: "How long until the harvest?" They answered: "Twenty days."

Then Jesus said: "See to it that these twenty days we wait, giving ourselves to fasting and prayer, for God will have mercy on you. Truly I say to you, God has given this dearth because here began the madness of men and the sin of Israel, when they said that I was God, or son of God."

Having fasted nineteen days, on the morning of the twentieth they saw the fields and hills covered with ripe grain. So they ran to Jesus and told him everything; and when Jesus heard it, he gave thanks to God and said: "Go, brothers, and gather the bread that God has given you." The men gathered so much grain that they did not know where to store it, and it was a cause of abundance in Israel.

The citizens took counsel to make Jesus their king; knowing which, he fled from them, and the disciples strove fifteen days to find him.

## Chapter 139


When Jesus was found by him who writes, and James with John — who weeping said: "O master, why did you flee? Grieving, we have sought you, and weeping all the disciples seek you" — Jesus answered: "I fled because I knew a company of devils was preparing for me that which in a short time you shall see. For the chief priests shall rise up against me with the elders of the people, and they shall take power from the Roman governor to kill me, because they fear that I will usurp the kingdom over Israel. But what is more: I shall be sold and betrayed by one of my disciples, as Joseph was sold in Egypt. But God, the just, shall make him fall, as the prophet David says: he shall make him fall into the pit who lays the snare for his neighbor — because God shall save me from their hands and shall take me from the world."

The three disciples feared, whom Jesus comforted, saying: "Do not fear, for none of you shall betray me," at which they received some consolation. The following day there came, two by two, thirty-six of Jesus' disciples; yet they settled in Damascus awaiting the others. And every one was grieving, for they knew that Jesus must depart from the world. So he opened his mouth and said: "Unhappy indeed is he who walks without knowing where he is to arrive; but much more unhappy is he who, able and knowing how to arrive at a good lodging, desires and wills to halt upon the road full of mud, in the rain, and in peril of robbers. Tell me, brothers: is this world our country? Surely not, for the first man was cast out into the world as into exile, wherein he suffers the penalty of his error. Shall there be found an exile who does not aspire to go to the rich country, finding himself in poverty? Assuredly, reason denies it, but experience proves it — for the lovers of the world do not wish to think upon death; rather, when they are spoken to of it, they do not wish to hear it spoken of."

## Chapter 140


"Believe you, O men, that I am come into the world with a privilege that no man has ever had, nor shall the messenger of God have it either? Seeing that our God did not create man to set him in the world, but indeed to place him in paradise.

"Assuredly, he who hopes to receive nothing from the Romans, because they are of a law foreign to him, will not leave his country with all he has, never to come back again, and go to dwell at Rome — and much less would he do it when he found himself to have offended Caesar. Even so I say to you in truth, and Solomon, prophet of God, cries with me: 'O death, how bitter is the memory of you to them that have rest in their riches!'

"I do not say this because I have to die now, seeing that I am sure to live even close to the end of the world. But I will speak to you of this so that you may learn to die.

"As God lives — everything that is done amiss even once is done ill; so to work a thing well, it is necessary to exercise oneself therein. Have you seen the soldiers, how in time of peace they exercise themselves among themselves as if they were at war? But how shall that man die a good death who has not learned to die well? 'Precious is the death of the saints in the presence of God,' said the prophet David. Do you know why? I will tell you: it is because, as all rare things are precious, so — the death of those who die well being rare — their death is precious before God our creator.

"Assuredly, everything that man begins, he not only wills to finish, but he strives that his intent may have a good end. O miserable man, who prizes his hose more than himself! For when he cuts the cloth he diligently measures before he cuts the cloth, and when it is cut he sews it with diligence. But his life, which is born to die — he alone dies not who is not born — for what reason will men not measure their life by death? Have you seen those who build, who for every stone they lay have the foundation as their end, measuring whether it is straight so that the wall does not fall? O miserable man, with what utter ruin shall the building of his life fall, because he looks not to the foundation of death!"

## Chapter 141


"Tell me: when man is born, how is he born? Assuredly he is born naked; and when he is laid dead under the earth, what advantage has he? A poor linen shroud wherein he is wrapped, and this is the reward that the world gives him.

"Now, if the means in every work must be proportionate to the beginning and the end, that the work may attain a good end, what end now shall the man have who desires earthly riches? He shall die, as David, prophet of God, says: 'The sinner shall die a most evil death.'

"If a man who sewed cloth should put beams in place of thread in the needle to sew the garments, how would the work attain its end? Assuredly he would work in vain and would be mocked by his neighbors. Now man sees not that he does this continually when he gathers earthly goods; for death is the needle, which the beams of earthly goods cannot pass. Nevertheless the fool continually strives to make the work succeed — but in vain. And whoever does not believe this at my word, let him look upon the tombs, for there he shall find the truth. He who would become wiser than all others, with fear of God, let him study the book of the tomb, for there he shall find the true doctrine for his salvation — for he will know how to beware of the world, the flesh, and the sense, seeing human flesh kept as food for worms.

"Tell me: if there were a road so conditioned that, walking in the middle of it, man would go safely, but walking on the extreme parts he would break his head — what would you say, seeing men oppose one another and try to outdo each other in getting nearest to the edges to kill themselves? What amazement would be yours! Assuredly you would say that they are mad and frenzied, and if they are not frenzied, they are desperate."

"Even so it is true," answered the disciples.

Then Jesus, weeping, said: "Truly such indeed are the lovers of the world; for if they lived according to reason — which holds the middle place in man — they would follow the law of God and would be saved from eternal death. But because they follow the flesh and the world, they are frenzied and cruel enemies of themselves, striving to live more arrogantly and more lasciviously one than the other."

## Chapter 142


When Judas the traitor saw that Jesus was fled, he lost the hope of becoming powerful in the world, for he kept Jesus' purse, in which was kept all that was given him for love of God. He hoped that Jesus would become king of Israel, and so he would become a powerful man. Wherefore, having lost this hope, he said within himself: "If this man were a prophet, he would know that I steal his money, and so he would lose patience and would cast me out of his service, knowing that I do not believe in him. And if he were a wise man, he would not flee the honor that God willed to give him. Wherefore it will be better that I come to terms with the chief priests and with the scribes and Pharisees, and see about giving him into their hands, that so I may obtain something good."

So, having made his resolution, he gave word to the scribes and Pharisees how the matter had passed in Nain. And they took counsel with the high priest, saying: "What shall we do if this man become king? Assuredly we shall fare badly, for he will reform the worship of God according to the ancient custom, for he cannot bear our traditions. Now what shall we do under the empire of such a man? Assuredly we shall perish, all of us with our children; for being cast out of our office we shall have to beg our bread. We, praised be God, now have a king and a governor who are strangers to our law, who care nothing for our law, as we care nothing for theirs. So we can do what we will; for even if we sin, our God is so merciful that with sacrifice and fasting he is appeased. But this man, becoming king, will not be appeased unless he see the worship of God as Moses writes it. And what is worse — he says that the Messiah shall not come of the stock of David, as one of his principal disciples has told us; but he says that he shall come of the stock of Ishmael, and that the promise was made in Ishmael and not in Isaac. Now what fruit shall be born if this man is left to live? Assuredly the Ishmaelites will come into repute with the Romans, and they will give them possession of our land; so Israel shall again be subjected to servitude, as it has been in the past."

So, when the pontiff heard the proposal, he answered that it was necessary to treat of it with Herod and with the governor, for the people are so inclined to him that without the soldiery we shall not be able to do anything; and may it please God that with the soldiery we may carry through this business. So, having taken counsel among themselves, they decided to take him by night, when the governor and Herod would lay hands on it.

## Chapter 143


When all the disciples had come to Damascus by the will of God, Judas the traitor on that day, more than any other, made a show of having grieved at Jesus' absence. So Jesus said: "Let every one beware of him who without occasion labours to give you tokens of love." And God took away our understanding, that we might not know to what end he said this.

After the coming of all the disciples, Jesus said: "Let us return into Galilee, for thus has the angel of God said to me, that I needs must go thither." So one sabbath morning Jesus came to Nazareth. When the townsfolk recognized Jesus, every one desired to see him. Now a publican by name Zacchaeus, of small stature, not being able to see Jesus for the great multitude, climbed up into a sycamore, and there waited for Jesus to pass by that place when he went to the synagogue. When Jesus came to that place, he lifted up his eyes and said: "Zacchaeus, come down, for to-day I will abide in your house." The man came down and received him with gladness, making a splendid feast.

The Pharisees murmured, saying to Jesus' disciples: "Why is your master gone in to eat with publicans and sinners?" Jesus answered: "For what cause does the physician [enter] into a house? Tell me, and I will tell you wherefore I am come in here." They answered: "To heal the sick." "You say the truth," said Jesus, "for the whole have no need of medicine, only the sick."

## Chapter 144


"As God lives, in whose presence my soul stands: God sends his prophets and his servants into the world, that sinners may repent; and he sends not for the sake of the righteous, for they have no need of repentance, even as he that is clean has no need of the bath. But truly I say to you, if you were true Pharisees you would be glad that I am gone in to sinners for their salvation.

"Tell me, know you your origin, and wherefore the world began to receive Pharisees? Surely I will tell you, seeing you know it not; wherefore hearken to my words. Enoch, a friend of God, who walked with God in truth, making no account of the world, was translated into paradise; and there he abides until the judgment, for when the end of the world draws nigh he shall return to the world with Elijah and one other. And men, having knowledge of this, through desire of paradise began to seek God their creator. For 'Pharisee' strictly means 'seek God' in the language of Canaan, for there did this name begin, in derision of good men, seeing that the Canaanites were given up to idolatry, which is the worship of human hands. Whereupon the Canaanites, beholding those of our people that were separated from the world to serve God, in derision, when they saw such an one, said 'Pharisee!' — that is, 'He seeketh God'; as much as to say: 'O mad fellow, you have no statues of idols and adorest the wind; wherefore look to your fate and come and serve our gods.'

"Truly I say to you," said Jesus, "all the saints and prophets of God have been Pharisees — not in name, as you are, but in deed. For in all their acts they sought God their creator, and for love of God they forsook cities and their own goods, selling them and giving to the poor for love of God."

## Chapter 145


"As God lives: in the time of Elijah, friend and prophet of God, there were twelve mountains inhabited by seventeen thousand Pharisees; and so it was that in so great a number not a single reprobate was found, but all were elect of God. But now, when Israel has more than a hundred thousand Pharisees, may it please God that out of every thousand there be one elect!"

The Pharisees answered in indignation: "So then we are all reprobate, and you hold our religion in reprobation!" Jesus answered: "I hold not in reprobation but in approbation the religion of true Pharisees, and for that I am ready to die. But come, let us see if you be Pharisees. Elijah, the friend of God, at the prayer of his disciple Elisha, wrote a little book, wherein he included all human wisdom with the law of God our Lord."

The Pharisees were confounded when they heard the book of Elijah named, for by their traditions they knew that no one observed such doctrine; wherefore they were fain to depart under pretext of having business to do. Then said Jesus: "If you were Pharisees, you would forsake all other business to attend to this, for the Pharisee seeks God alone." In confusion, then, they tarried to listen to Jesus, who said again: "To Elijah, servant of God" — for so begins the little book — "to all them that desire to walk with God their creator, writes this:

"Whoso desires to learn much, little do they fear God, for he who fears God is content to know only that which God wills.

"They that seek fair words seek not God, who does naught but reprove our sins.

"They that desire to seek God, let them shut fast the doors and windows of their house, for the master suffers not himself to be found outside his house, where he is not loved. Guard therefore your senses and guard your heart, for God is not found outside of us, in this world wherein he is hated.

"They that wish to do good works, let them attend to their own selves, for it boots nothing to gain the whole world and lose one's own soul.

"They that wish to teach others, let them live better than others, for nothing can be learned from him who knows less than ourselves. How, then, shall the sinner amend his life when he hears one worse than he teaching him?

"They that seek God, let them flee the conversation of men; for Moses, being alone upon Mount Sinai, found him, and spoke with God as does a friend who speaks with a friend.

"They that seek God, once only in thirty days shall they go forth where be men of the world; for in one day can be done works for two years in respect of the business of him that seeks God.

"When he walks, let him not look save at his own feet. When he speaks, let him not speak save what is necessary.

"When they eat, let them rise from the table still hungry; thinking every day not to reach the next; spending their time as one draws his breath.

"Let one garment, of the skin of beasts, suffice. Let the lump of earth sleep on the naked earth; for every night let two hours of sleep suffice. Let him hate no one save himself; let him condemn no one save himself.

"In prayer, let them stand in such fear as if they were at the judgment to come.

"Now do this in the service of God, with the law that God has given you through Moses; for in such wise shall you find God, that in every time and place you shall feel that you are in God and God in you."

"This is the little book of Elijah, O Pharisees; wherefore again I say to you, that if you were Pharisees you would have had joy that I am entered in here, for God has mercy upon sinners."

## Chapter 146


Then said Zacchaeus: "Sir, behold I will give, for love of God, fourfold all that I have received by usury." Then said Jesus: "This day is salvation come to this house. Truly, truly, many publicans, harlots, and sinners shall go into the kingdom of God, and they that account themselves righteous shall go into the eternal flames."

Hearing this, the Pharisees departed in indignation. Then said Jesus to them that were converted to repentance, and to his disciples: "There was a father of a family who had two sons, and the younger said: 'Father, give me my portion of goods'; and his father gave it him. And he, having received his portion, departed and went into a far country, whereupon he wasted all his substance with harlots, living luxuriously. After this there arose a great famine in that country, insomuch that the wretched man went to serve a citizen, who set him to feed swine in his property. And while feeding them he assuaged his hunger in company with the swine, eating the acorns of the oak. But when he came to himself he said: 'Oh, how many in my father's house have abundance in feasting, and I perish here with hunger! I will arise, therefore, and will go to my father, and will say to him: Father, I have sinned in heaven against you; do with me as you do to one of your servants.'

"The poor man went; whereupon it came to pass that his father saw him coming from afar off, and was moved to compassion over him. So he went forth to meet him, and having come up to him he embraced him and kissed him. The son bowed himself down, saying: 'Father, I have sinned in heaven against you; do with me as with one of your servants, for I am not worthy to be called your son.' The father answered: 'Say not so, O son, for you are my son, and I will not suffer you to stand in the state of my servant.' And he called his servants and said: 'Bring hither new robes and clothe this my son, and give him new hose; give him the ring on his finger, and straightway kill the fatted calf, and we will make merry. For this my son was dead and is now risen again; he was lost and now is found.'"

## Chapter 147


While they were making merry in the house, behold the elder son came home, and hearing that they were making merry within, marvelled; and having called one of the servants, he asked him wherefore they were in such wise making merry. The servant answered him: "Your brother is come, and your father has killed the fatted calf, and they are feasting." The elder son was greatly angered when he heard this, and would not go into the house. Therefore his father came out to him and said to him: "Son, your brother is come; come you therefore and rejoice with him."

The son answered with indignation: "I have ever served you with good service, and yet you never gave me a lamb to eat with my friends. But as for this worthless fellow that departed from you, wasting all his portion with harlots, now that he is come you have killed the fatted calf."

The father answered: "Son, you are ever with me, and everything is yours; but this one was dead and is alive again, was lost and now is found; therefore we needs must rejoice."

The elder son was the more angry, and said: "Go you and triumph, for I will not eat at the table of fornicators." And he departed from his father without receiving even a piece of money.

"As God lives," said Jesus, "even so is there rejoicing made by the angels of God over one sinner that does penance."

And when they had eaten, he departed, for he was minded to go into Judea. Then said the disciples: "Master, go not into Judea, for we know that against you the Pharisees with the high priest have taken counsel." Jesus answered: "I know it before they did it, but I fear not, for against the will of God they can do nothing; wherefore let them do what they will, for I fear them not, but I fear God."

## Chapter 148


Now tell me: the Pharisees of today — are they Pharisees? Are they servants of God? Certainly not. But I say to you in truth that there is no worse thing here on earth than this: that a man cover himself with profession and religious habit to cover his wickedness. I will tell you only one example of the ancient Pharisees, so that you may know the present ones. After the departure of Elijah, because of the great persecution by the idolaters, that holy congregation of Pharisees was scattered. For in that same time of Elijah, in one year, more than ten thousand prophets were slain, who were true Pharisees.

Two Pharisees went into the mountains to dwell there, and the one abode fifteen years knowing nought of his companion, although they were but one hour's journey apart. See, then, if they were inquisitive! It came to pass that a drought arose in those mountains, whereupon both set themselves to seek water, and so they found each other. Whereupon the more aged said — for it was their custom that the eldest should speak before every other, and they held it a great sin for a young man to speak before an old one — the elder, I say, said: "Where dwellest you, brother?"

He answered, pointing out the dwelling to him with his finger: "Here I dwell" — for they were nigh to the dwelling of the younger.

Said the elder: "How long is it, O brother, that you dwellest here?"

The younger answered: "Fifteen years."

Said the elder: "Perchance you came when Ahab slew the servants of God?"

"Even so," answered the younger.

Said the elder: "O brother, know you who is now king of Israel?"

The younger answered: "O brother, it is God who is king of Israel, for the idolaters do not reign over Israel but persecute it."

"It is true," said the elder, "but I meant to say: who is it now that persecutes Israel?"

The younger answered: "The sins of Israel persecute Israel; for if they had not sinned, he would not send against Israel the idolatrous princes."

Then said the elder: "Who is that infidel prince whom God has given for the chastisement of Israel?"

The younger answered: "Now how should I know, seeing these fifteen years I have seen no man save you, and I know not how to read, wherefore no letters are sent to me?"

Said the elder: "Now, how new your sheepskins be! Who has given them to you, if you have not seen any man?"

## Chapter 149


The younger answered: "He who kept good the raiment of the people of Israel for forty years in the wilderness has kept my skins even as you see."

Then the elder perceived that the younger was more perfect than he, for he had every year had dealings with men. Whereupon, in order to have his conversation, he said: "Brother, you know not how to read, and I know how to read, and in my house I have the psalms of David. Come, then, that every day I may give you a lesson, declaring to you what David says."

The younger answered: "Let us go now."

Said the elder: "O brother, it is now two days since I have drunk water; let us therefore seek a little water."

The younger answered: "O brother, it is now two months since I have drunk water. Let us go, therefore, and see what God says by his prophet David: the Lord is able to give us water."

Whereupon they returned to the dwelling of the elder, at the door whereof they found a spring of living water.

Said the elder: "O brother, you are a holy one of God; for your sake has God given this spring."

The younger answered: "O brother, in humility say you this; but certain it is that if God had done this for my sake, he would have made a spring close to my dwelling, that I should not have departed. For I confess to you that I sinned against you when you saidst that for two days you hadst not drunk you soughtest water, and I had been two months without drinking, whereupon in my mind I felt exaltation, as though I were better than you."

Then said the elder: "O brother, you saidst the truth, therefore you didst not sin."

Said the younger: "O brother, you have forgotten what our father Elijah said: that he who seeketh God ought only to condemn himself. Assuredly he wrote it not that we might know it, but rather that we might observe it."

Said the more aged, perceiving the truth and the righteousness of his companion: "It is true; and our God has pardoned you."

And having said this he took the Psalms, and read that which our father David says: "I will set a watch over my mouth, that my tongue decline not into words of malice, excusing with excuse my sins." And here the aged man made a discourse upon the tongue, and the younger departed. Whereupon they were fifteen years more ere they found one another, because the younger changed his dwelling.

When, then, he had found him again, the elder said: "O brother, wherefore returnedst you not to my dwelling?"

The younger answered: "Because I have not yet learned well what you saidst to me."

Then said the elder: "How can this be, seeing fifteen years are past?"

The younger answered: "As for the words, I learned them in a single hour and have never forgotten them; but I have not yet observed them. To what purpose, then, is it to learn much and not to observe it? Our God seeketh not that our intellect should be good, but rather our heart. So on the day of judgement he will not ask us what we have learned, but what we have done."

## Chapter 150


The elder answered: "O brother, say not so, for you despise knowledge, which our God wills to be prized."

The younger answered: "Now, how shall I speak now so as not to fall into sin, for your word is true, and mine also. I say, then, that they who know the commandments of God written in the Law ought to observe them, if they would afterwards learn more. And all that a man learns, let it be to observe it, and not merely to know it."
